“是的，”士兵答道，“愿上帝赐福他们，再没有比他更好的军官了，他对我们大家都好；至于朱莉娅小姐，穷苦人差不多把她当神明敬着呢。”

“慈悲的上天！”夏洛特喊道，“那么蒙特维尔只对我一个人不义么？”

士兵随即指给她克雷顿上校家的门；她心头突突地跳着，叩门求进。[17]

## XXXI18 Subject Continued / XXXI18 承前章

===Original===

When the door was opened, Charlotte, in a voice rendered scarcely articulate, through cold and the extreme agitation of her mind, demanded whether Mrs. Crayton was at home; the servant hesitated: he knew that his lady was engaged at a game of picquet with her dear Corydon, nor could he think she would like to be disturbed by a person whose appearance spoke her of so little consequence as Charlotte; yet there was something in her countenance that rather interested him in her favor, and he said his lady was engaged; but if she had any particular message he would deliver it.

“Take up this letter,” said Charlotte, “tell her the unhappy writer of it waits in the hall for an answer.”

The tremulous accent, the tearful eye, must have moved any heart not composed of adamant. The man took the letter from the poor suppliant, and hastily ascended the staircase.

“A letter, madam,” said he, presenting it to his lady; “an immediate answer is required.”

===Chinese===

门开了，夏洛特用一副被寒冷和心绪的极度激动弄得几乎不成声的嗓音，问克雷顿夫人在不在家；仆人犹豫了：他知道夫人和她亲爱的科里冬正打着皮克牌，也想不出她会乐意被一个外表看来无足轻重（如夏洛特这般）的人打扰；可她脸上有种东西颇令他心生怜惜，于是他说夫人正有事；倘若她有特别的话要转达，他可以代呈。

“把这封信呈上去，”夏洛特说，“告诉她，写信的不幸人就在门厅里候着回音。”

那颤动的声调，那含泪的眼睛，除非是铁石的心，没有不被打动的。仆人从可怜的哀求者手里接过信，匆匆上了楼。

“一封信，夫人，”他把信呈给女主人说；“要即刻回音。”

===Original===

Mrs. Crayton glanced her eye carelessly over the contents. “What stuff is this?” cried she, haughtily; “have not I told you a thousand times that I will not be plagued with beggars and petitions from people one knows nothing about? Go tell the woman I can’t do anything in it. I’m sorry, but one can’t relieve everybody.”

The servant bowed, and heavily returned with this chilling message to Charlotte.

“Surely,” said she, “Mrs. Crayton has not read my letter. Go, my good friend, pray, go back to her; tell her it is Charlotte Temple who requests beneath her hospitable roof to find shelter from the inclemency of the season.”

“Prithee, don’t plague me, man,” cried Mrs. Crayton, impatiently, as the servant advanced something in behalf of the unhappy girl. “I tell you I don’t know her.”

===Chinese===

克雷顿夫人漫不经心地扫了一眼信的内容。“这是什么玩意儿？”她傲慢地喊道；“我不是跟你说过一千遍了么，那些不知打哪儿来的人的乞讨和呈文，别拿来烦我？去告诉那个女人，这事我帮不上忙。很抱歉，可人总不能救济每一个人。”

仆人躬了躬身，带着这句冷冰冰的回话，脚步沉重地回到夏洛特那里。

“想必克雷顿夫人没有读我的信。去，我的好朋友，求你了，回去告诉她；是夏洛特·坦普尔，求在您好客的屋顶下，寻一个蔽身之所，躲一躲这严冬的酷寒。”

“求你别烦我，”仆人正要替那不幸的姑娘说句什么，克雷顿夫人不耐烦地喊道，“我告诉你，我不认识她。”

===Original===

“Not know me!” cried Charlotte, rushing into the room (for she had followed the man upstairs), “not know me﻿—not remember the ruined Charlotte Temple, who, but for you, perhaps might still have been innocent, still have been happy. Oh! La Rue, this is beyond everything I could have believed possible.”

“Upon my honor, miss,” replied the unfeeling woman with the utmost effrontery, “this is a most unaccountable address: it is beyond my comprehension. John,” continued she, turning to the servant, “the young woman is certainly out of her senses; do pray take her away, she terrifies me to death.”

“Oh, God!” cried Charlotte, clasping her hands in an agony, “this is too much; what will become of me? but I will not leave you; they shall not tear me from you; here on my knees I conjure you to save me from perishing in the street; if you really have forgotten me, O, for charity’s sweet sake, this night let me be sheltered from the winter’s piercing cold.”

The kneeling figure of Charlotte, in her affecting situation, might have moved the heart of a stoic to compassion; but Mrs. Crayton remained inflexible. In vain did Charlotte recount the time they had known each other at Chichester; in vain mention their being in the same ship; in vain were the names of Montraville and Belcour mentioned. Mrs. Crayton could only say she was sorry for her imprudence, but could not think of having her own reputation endangered by encouraging a woman of that kind in her own house; besides, she did not know what trouble and expense she might bring upon her husband by giving shelter to a woman in her situation.

===Chinese===

“不认识我！”夏洛特喊道，冲进房来（她一直跟着那人上了楼），“不认识我——不记得这被毁了的夏洛特·坦普尔，若不是你，她或许至今清白，至今幸福。哦！拉吕，这超出了我所能相信的一切。”

“我以名誉担保，小姐，”那无情的女人带着极度的厚颜答道，“这真是一篇莫名其妙的话：我全然不懂。约翰，”她转向仆人，接着说，“这年轻女人准是疯了；千万把她带走，她吓得我要死。”

“哦，上帝！”夏洛特苦痛地绞着双手喊道，“这太过分了；我可怎么办？可我不离开你；他们休想把我从你身边扯开；我跪在这里求你，救我不要死在街头；倘若你真的忘了我，哦，看在善心的甜蜜名分上，今夜也让我避一避这刺骨的严寒吧。”

夏洛特跪着的身影，处身那样动人的境地，本可以打动斯多噶哲人的恻隐之心；可克雷顿夫人依然不为所动。夏洛特细数他们在奇切斯特相识的时光，是徒然；提起她们同乘一条船，是徒然；提起蒙特维尔和贝尔库尔的名字，也是徒然。克雷顿夫人只会说，她为她的轻率遗憾，却决不能想望容这样一个女子住进自己家里，拿自己的名誉去冒险；况且，收留一个处于她这种境地的女子，还不知要给她丈夫招来多少麻烦和开销。

===Original===

“I can at least die here,” said Charlotte. “I feel I can not long survive this dreadful conflict. Father of mercy, here let me finish my existence.” Her agonizing sensations overpowered her, and she fell senseless on the floor.

“Take her away,” said Mrs. Crayton; “she will really frighten me into hysterics; take her away, I say, this instant.”

“And where must I take the poor creature?” said the servant, with a voice and look of compassion.

“Anywhere,” cried she hastily, “only don’t let me ever see her again. I declare she has flurried me so, I shan’t be myself again this fortnight.”

John, assisted by his fellow servant, raised and carried her downstairs. “Poor soul,” said he, “you shall not lay in the street this night. I have a bed and a poor little hovel, where my wife and her little ones rest them; but they shall watch tonight and you shall be sheltered from danger.” They placed her in a chair; and the benevolent man, assisted by one of his comrades, carried her to the place where his wife and children lived. A surgeon was sent for; he bled her; she gave signs of returning life, and before the dawn gave birth to a female infant. After this event, she lay for some hours in a kind of stupor; and, if at any time she spoke, it was with a quickness and incoherence that plainly evinced the total deprivation of her reason.


===Chinese===

“我至少可以死在这里，”夏洛特说。“我觉得我捱不过这可怕的冲突了。慈悲的天父，就让我在这里了结我的生命吧。”极痛的感觉压倒了她，她失去了知觉，倒在地板上。

“把她抬走，”克雷顿夫人说；“她真要把我吓出歇斯底里了；把她抬走，我说，立刻。”

“那叫我把这可怜人抬到哪里去呢？”仆人说，声音和面容都带着怜悯。

“哪儿都行，”她慌忙喊道，“只要别让我再看见她。我敢说，她把我搅得这样心慌，半个月都缓不过来。”

约翰在同伴的帮助下，把她抱起来抬下楼。“可怜的人，”他说，“今夜你不会睡街上了。我有一张床、一座破旧的小屋，我老婆和孩子们都在那里歇息；今夜叫她们守着你，你不会有危险。”他们把她安置在椅子上；这好心的人由他的一名同伴帮着，把她抬到他妻儿住的地方。请来了外科医生；给她放了血；她有了回生的迹象，天亮之前，产下了一个女婴。此后，她躺着，几个小时处于一种昏迷之中；偶尔开口，也是那样急促而语无伦次，显然全然丧失了理智。

## XXXII Reasons Why and Wherefore / XXXII 缘由究竟

===Original===

The reader of sensibility may perhaps be astonished to find Mrs. Crayton could so positively deny any knowledge of Charlotte; it is, therefore, but just that her conduct should in some measure be accounted for.

She had ever been fully sensible of the superiority of Charlotte’s sense and virtue; she was conscious that she had never swerved from rectitude had it not been for her bad precepts and worse example. These were things as yet unknown to her husband; and she wished not to have that part of her conduct exposed to him, as she had great reason to fear she had already lost considerable part of that power she once maintained over him. She trembled whilst Charlotte was in the house, lest the colonel should return; she perfectly well remembered how much he seemed interested in her favor, whilst on their passage from England, and made no doubt but, should he see her in her present distress, he would offer her an asylum, and protect her to the utmost of his power. In that case, she feared the unguarded nature of Charlotte might discover to the colonel the part she had taken in the unhappy girl’s elopement, and she well knew the contrast between her own and Charlotte’s conduct, would make the former appear in no very respectable light. Had she reflected properly, she would have afforded the poor girl protection; and, by enjoining her silence, insured it by acts of repeated kindness; but vice in general blinds its votaries, and they discover their real characters to the world when they are most studious to preserve appearances.

Just so it happened with Mrs. Crayton: her servants made no scruple of mentioning the cruel conduct of their lady to a poor distressed lunatic who claimed her protection; everyone joined in reprobating her inhumanity; nay, even Corydon thought she might at least have ordered her to be taken care of, but he dare not even hint it to her, for he lived but in her smiles, and drew from her lavish fondness large sums to support an extravagance to which the state of his own finances was very inadequate: it can not therefore be supposed that he wished Mrs. Crayton to be very liberal in her bounty to the afflicted suppliant; yet vice had not so entirely seared over his heart but the sorrows of Charlotte could find a vulnerable part.

Charlotte had now been three days with her humane preservers, but she was totally insensible of everything; she raved incessantly for Montraville and her father; she was not conscious of being a mother, nor took the least notice of her child, except to ask whose it was, and why it was not carried to its parents.

===Chinese===

心思敏感的读者也许会诧异，克雷顿夫人竟能这样斩钉截铁地否认认识夏洛特；因此，把她的行为多少解释一番，才算公道。

她一向深知夏洛特的见识与美德高出自己；她心里明白，若不是自己坏的说教和更坏的榜样，夏洛特本不会偏离正路。这些事她丈夫还一无所知；她不愿自己行为的这一面暴露在他眼前，因为她大有理由担心，她从前在他身上维持的那份权柄，已经失去了相当一部分。夏洛特在屋里的时候，她一直发抖，惟恐上校回来；她清清楚楚记得，从英格兰来的航程中，他显得多么关切夏洛特的好处，也毫不怀疑，他若见她落在眼前的苦难里，定会给她一个庇护所，竭尽所能保护她。果真如此，她便怕夏洛特不设防的天性，会把她在那不幸姑娘私奔中所扮演的角色泄露给上校；她也深知，自己的行为同夏洛特的行为一对照，前者决显不出什么体面来。她若思虑得当，本该给那可怜的姑娘保护；再叮嘱她缄默，用再三的善举把这缄默稳稳买定；可罪恶总使它的信徒盲目，他们越是用心维持体面，越把真实的面目暴露给世人。

克雷顿夫人正是如此：她的仆人们毫不顾忌地议论女主人对一个求她保护的、可怜的疯癫女子何等残忍；人人都加入谴责她的不仁；不止如此，连科里冬也想，她至少该吩咐人照料她一下，可他连暗示也不敢向她暗示，因为他靠她的笑容活着，靠她慷慨的宠爱支取大笔的钱，去维持一种他自己的财力远远不逮的挥霍：所以不能指望他愿意见克雷顿夫人对这苦难的哀求者太慷慨；然而罪恶还不曾把他心完全烧成铁石，夏洛特的悲伤仍找得到脆弱之处。

夏洛特在她仁慈的救护者家里已住了三天，可她对一切都全然无知觉；她不停地喊着蒙特维尔和她父亲；她不觉得自己做了母亲，对孩子的存在毫不在意，只问那是谁家的孩子，为什么不送还给它的父母。

===Original===

“Oh!” said she one day, starting up on hearing the infant cry, “why, why, will you keep that child here; I am sure you would not if you knew how hard it was for a mother to be parted from her infant: it is like tearing the cords of life asunder. Oh! could you see the horrid sight which I now behold﻿—there﻿—there stands my dear mother, her poor bosom bleeding at every vein; her gentle, affectionate heart torn in a thousand pieces, and all for the loss of a ruined, ungrateful child. Save me﻿—save me﻿—from her frown! I dare not﻿—indeed I dare not speak to her.”

Such were the dreadful images that haunted her distracted mind, and nature was sinking fast under the dreadful malady which medicine had no power to remove. The surgeon who attended her was a humane man; he exerted his utmost abilities to save her; but he saw she was in want of many necessaries and comforts which the poverty of her hospitable host rendered him unable to provide; he therefore determined to make her situation known to some of the officers’ ladies, and endeavor to make a collection for her relief.

When he returned home after making this resolution, he found a message from Mrs. Beauchamp, who had just arrived from Rhode Island, requesting he would call and see one of her children, who was very unwell. “I do not know,” said he, as he was hastening to obey the summons, “I do not know a woman to whom I could apply with more hope of success than Mrs. Beauchamp. I will endeavor to interest her in this poor girl’s behalf; she wants the soothing balm of friendly consolation: we may perhaps save her; we will try, at least.”

“And where is she?” cried Mrs. Beauchamp, when he had prescribed something for the child, and told his little pathetic tale, “where is she, sir? we will go to her immediately. Heaven forbid that I should be deaf to the calls of humanity. Come, we will go this instant.” Then seizing the doctor’s arm, they sought the habitation that contained the dying Charlotte.


===Chinese===

“哦！”一天，她听见婴儿啼哭，猛地坐起来说，“为什么，为什么你们把这孩子留在这里；你们若知道母子分离有多苦，就决不会这样了：那就像把生命的琴弦生生扯断。哦！你们若能看见我此刻见的可怕景象——那里——那里站着我亲爱的母亲，她可怜的胸膛条条血管都在流血；她那温柔慈爱的心撕成了一千片，全是为了一个毁了 的、忘恩的孩子的丢失。救救我——救救我——别叫我看见她皱眉！我不敢——真的不敢同她说话。”

缠住她纷乱心神的，便是这些可怕的形象；天性在那医药无力回天的恶疾之下迅速沉落。照看她的外科医生是个仁厚的人；他竭尽所能救她；可他看出她缺少许多必需品和慰藉，而她那位好客的房东又穷得无力供给；于是他决意把她的境况告知几位军官夫人，设法为她募一点救济。

下了这个决心回到家，他发现博尚夫人差人送来的一封信——她刚从罗得岛回来，请他去看她的一个孩子，孩子很不安适。“我不知道，”他应召匆匆出门时想，“不知道还有哪位夫人，能比博尚夫人更让我有成功的指望。我要设法让她关心这可怜的姑娘；她需要友情的安慰的香膏：我们或许还救得了她；至少，我们要试一试。”

“她在哪里？”他给孩子开了药、讲完这段小小的伤心故事之后，博尚夫人喊道，“她在哪里，先生？我们这就去她那里。上天不容我对人性的呼唤充耳不闻。来，我们即刻就去。”说着她挽起医生的手臂，两人寻到了那座住着垂死的夏洛特的居所。

## XXXIII Which People Void of Feeling Need Not Read / XXXIII 无情之人不必读这一章

===Original===

When Mrs. Beauchamp entered the apartment of the poor sufferer, she started back with horror. On a wretched bed, without hangings and but poorly supplied with covering, lay the emaciated figure of what still retained the semblance of a lovely woman, though sickness had so altered her features that Mrs. Beauchamp had not the least recollection of her person. In one corner of the room stood a woman washing, and shivering over a small fire, two healthy, but half-naked children; the infant was asleep beside its mother, and on a chair by the bedside stood a porringer and wooden spoon containing a little gruel, and a teacup with about two spoonfuls of wine in it. Mrs. Beauchamp had never before beheld such a scene of poverty; she shuddered involuntarily, and exclaiming﻿—“heaven preserve us!” leaned on the back of a chair, ready to sink to the earth. The doctor repented having so precipitately brought her into this affecting scene; but there was no time for apologies: Charlotte caught the sound of her voice, and starting almost out of bed, exclaimed﻿—“Angel of peace and mercy, art thou come to deliver me? Oh, I know you are, for whenever you was near me I felt eased of half my sorrows; but you don’t know me, nor can I, with all the recollection I am mistress of, remember your name just now; but I know that benevolent countenance and the softness of that voice, which has so often comforted the wretched Charlotte.”

Mrs. Beauchamp had, during the time Charlotte was speaking, seated herself on the bed: and taking one of her hands she looked at her attentively, and at the name of Charlotte she perfectly conceived the whole shocking affair. A faint sickness came over her. “Gracious Heaven!” said she, “is this possible?” and bursting into tears, she reclined the burning head of Charlotte on her own bosom; and folding her arms about her, wept over her in silence. “Oh,” said Charlotte, “you are very good to weep thus for me: it is a long time since I shed a tear for myself: my head and heart are both on fire; but these tears of yours seem to cool and refresh it. Oh, now I remember you said you would send a letter to my poor father: do you think he ever received it? or perhaps you have brought me an answer: why don’t you speak, madam? Does he say I may go home? Well, he is very good: I shall soon be ready.”

She then made an effort to get out of bed; but being prevented, her frenzy again returned, and she raved with the greatest wildness and incoherence. Mrs. Beauchamp, finding it was impossible for her to be removed, contented herself with ordering the apartment to be made more comfortable, and procuring a proper nurse for both mother and child; and having learned the particulars of Charlotte’s fruitless application to Mrs. Crayton from honest John, she amply rewarded him for his benevolence, and returned home with a heart oppressed with many painful sensations, but yet rendered easy by the reflection that she had performed her duty toward a distressed fellow creature.

Early the next morning she again visited Charlotte, and found her tolerably composed; she called her by name, thanked her for her goodness, and when her child was brought to her, pressed it in her arms, wept over it, and called it the offspring of disobedience. Mrs. Beauchamp was delighted to see her so much amended, and began to hope she might recover, and in spite of her former errors, become a useful and respectable member of society; but the arrival of the doctor put an end to these delusive hopes: he said nature was making her last effort, and a few hours would most probably consign the unhappy girl to her kindred dust.

===Chinese===

博尚夫人走进那可怜的受难者的房间，惊怖得倒退了一步。一张破床上，没有床幔，盖的东西也稀薄，躺着一个瘦骨嶙峋的身形，虽还保留着一个可爱女子的模样，病魔却已把她的容貌改变得使博尚夫人全然认不出了。屋子一角，一个女人在洗衣，缩着身子守着一小盆火；两个健康却半裸的孩子；婴儿睡在母亲身旁；床头椅上放着一只粥碗和一把木匙，盛着一点麦片粥，还有一只茶杯，里面约有两匙酒。博尚夫人从未见过这样的贫穷景象；她不由自主地打了个寒颤，喊了一声——“上天保佑我们！”便扶住椅背，几乎要跌坐在地。医生懊悔不该这样仓促地带她进这伤心之地；可道歉已来不及：夏洛特听出了她的声音，几乎从床上跳起来，喊道——“和平与仁慈的天使，你是来解脱我的么？哦，我知道你是；因为你一靠近我，我的悲伤便消了一半；可你不认得我，而我呢，纵然竭力回想，此刻也想不起你的名字；可我认得那副仁厚的面容，认得那声音的温柔——它曾多少次安慰过不幸的夏洛特。”

夏洛特说话的时候，博尚夫人在床边坐下：执起她的一只手，端详着她；一听到夏洛特这个名字，她便把整个骇人的事想明白了。一阵轻微的昏眩涌上来。“慈悲的上天！”她说，“这怎么可能？”她泪如泉涌，把夏洛特烧灼的头搂在自己怀里；双臂环着她，默默流泪。“哦，”夏洛特说，“你这样为我哭，你真好：我自己已经很久不曾流泪了：我的头和心都在燃烧；可你这些泪似乎把它凉一凉、清爽清爽。哦，现在我想起来了，你说过要替我给我可怜的父亲寄一封信：你想他收到了么？或许你带来了回信：您怎么不说话，夫人？他说我可以回家了么？好，他真好：我很快就准备好了。”

随后她挣扎着要下床；被拦住了，她的狂乱又发作起来，喊叫得异常激烈而语无伦次。博尚夫人见移她不动，只得吩咐把屋子收拾得舒适些，并为母子二人雇了一名合适的看护；又从老实的约翰口中得知夏洛特向克雷顿夫人求助无果的详情，便重赏了他的仁厚，怀着满心沉重的痛楚回了家——却又因想到自己已对受苦的同胞尽了本分而心安。

第二天一早她又来看夏洛特，见她相当平静；她唤她的名字，谢她的好意；孩子抱到她跟前时，她把它搂在怀里，对着它流泪，称它为“不从的后代”。博尚夫人见她大有起色，很是欢喜，开始指望她或许能康复，抛开旧日的过失，重新做一个有用、可敬的人；可医生的到来破灭了这些虚妄的希望：他说天性在作最后的挣扎，不出几个钟头，这不幸的姑娘多半就要归于尘土了。

===Original===

Being asked how she found herself, she replied﻿—“Why, better, much better, doctor. I hope now I have but little more to suffer. I had last night a few hours sleep, and when I awoke recovered the full power of recollection. I am quite sensible of my weakness; I feel I have but little longer to combat with the shafts of affliction. I have an humble confidence in the mercy of Him who died to save the world, and trust that my sufferings in this state of mortality, joined to my unfeigned repentance, through His mercy, have blotted my offences from the sight of my offended Maker. I have but one care﻿—my poor infant! Father of mercy!” continued she, raising her eyes, “of Thy infinite goodness, grant that the sins of the parent be not visited on the unoffending child. May those who taught me to despise Thy laws be forgiven; lay not my offences to their charge I beseech Thee; and oh! shower the choicest of Thy blessings on those whose pity has soothed the afflicted heart, and made easy even the bed of pain and sickness.”

She was exhausted by this fervent address to the throne of mercy, and though her lips still moved, her voice became inarticulate: she lay for some time, as it were, in a doze, and then recovering, faintly pressed Mrs. Beauchamp’s hand, and requested that a clergyman might be sent for.

On his arrival she joined fervently in the pious office, frequently mentioning her ingratitude to her parents as what lay most heavy at her heart. When she had performed the last solemn duty, and was preparing to lie down, a little bustle on the outside door occasioned Mrs. Beauchamp to open it and inquire the cause. A man, in appearance about forty, presented himself, and asked for Mrs. Beauchamp.

“That is my name, sir,” said she.

===Chinese===

问她觉得怎样，她答道——“唔，好些了，好多了，医生。我希望如今我受的苦不多了。昨夜我睡了几个钟头，醒来时记忆完全恢复了。我很清楚自己的虚弱；我知道我与苦难的箭镞交战不了多久了。我谦卑地信托那位为拯救世人而死的者的慈悲，相信我在尘世的受难，加上我真诚的悔悟，凭着他的慈悲，已把我的过犯从我触犯的造物主眼前抹去了。我只挂着一件事——我可怜的孩子！慈悲的天父！”她举目接着说，“以你无限的良善，求你叫父母的罪不落在无辜的孩子身上。愿那些教我轻看你律法的人得蒙宽恕；求你不要把我的过犯归到他们头上；哦！把你最精选的福分，倾在那些以怜恤抚慰受苦者的心、甚至叫病痛之榻也得安舒的人身上。”

这一番向慈悲宝座发出的热切祷告使她力竭；她的嘴唇还在动，声音却不成语句了：她躺着，有一阵像是打盹，随后缓过来，轻轻握了握博尚夫人的手，请求派人去请一位牧师。

牧师到了，她热切地同他行了虔敬的礼，屡屡提到她对父母的忘恩，说那是压在她心上最重的东西。行完这最后的庄严本分，她正要躺下，外门一阵小小的骚动，使博尚夫人开门去看个究竟。一个看来约四十岁的男人站在那里，求见博尚夫人。

“那正是我的名字，先生。”她说。

===Original===

“Oh, then, my dear madam,” cried he, “tell me where I may find my poor, ruined, but repentant child.”

Mrs. Beauchamp was surprised and affected; she knew not what to say; she foresaw the agony this interview would occasion Mr. Temple, who had just arrived in search of his Charlotte, and yet was sensible that the pardon and blessing of her father would soften even the agonies of death to the daughter.

She hesitated. “Tell me, madam,” cried he, wildly, “tell me, I beseech thee, does she live? shall I see my darling once again? Perhaps she is in this house. Lead, lead me to her, that I may bless her, and then lie down and die.”

The ardent manner in which he uttered these words occasioned him to raise his voice. It caught the ear of Charlotte: she knew the beloved sound: and uttering a loud shriek, she sprang forward as Mr. Temple entered the room. “My adored father.” “My long lost child.” Nature could support no more, and they both sunk lifeless into the arms of the attendants.

===Chinese===

“哦，那么，亲爱的夫人，”他喊道，“告诉我，在哪里能找到我那可怜的、被毁的、却已悔悟的孩子。”

博尚夫人又惊又痛；她不知说什么好；她预见这次会面将给坦普尔先生带来怎样的苦楚——他刚寻着他的夏洛特赶到这里；可她又明白，父亲的宽恕和祝福，会把死亡的剧痛也化软。

她犹豫着。“告诉我，夫人，”他狂乱地喊道，“告诉我，我求你，她还活着么？我还能再见我的宝贝一面么？或许她就在这座房子里。领我去，领我去见她，好让我祝福她，然后躺下死去。”

他说这些话时的激烈，使他不自觉地提高了嗓音。那声音进了夏洛特的耳朵：她听出那亲爱的声息：坦普尔先生进门时，她一声尖呼，扑上前去。“我敬爱的父亲。”“我久失的孩子。”天性再支撑不住，两人双双昏死在侍者们的怀里。

===Original===

Charlotte was again put into bed, and a few moments restored Mr. Temple: but to describe the agony of his sufferings is past the power of anyone, who though they may readily conceive, can not delineate the dreadful scene. Every eye gave testimony of what each heart felt﻿—but all were silent.

When Charlotte recovered, she found herself supported in her father’s arms. She cast on him a most expressive look, but was unable to speak. A reviving cordial was administered. She then asked in a low voice for her child: it was brought to her: she put it in her father’s arms. “Protect her,” said she, “and bless your dying﻿—”

Unable to finish the sentence, she sunk back on her pillow: her countenance was serenely composed; she regarded her father as he pressed the infant to his breast, with a steadfast look; a sudden beam of joy passed across her languid features: she raised her eyes to heaven﻿—and then closed them forever.


===Chinese===

夏洛特重新被放上床，片刻之后坦普尔先生缓了过来：可他受苦的剧烈，不是任何人所能描画的——尽管理解不难，那可怕的一幕却无人能够勾画。每一只眼睛都为各人的心作证——但大家都沉默着。

夏洛特苏醒过来，发觉自己被父亲的手臂扶着。她投给他一个意味深长的眼色，却说不出话。人们给她灌下一剂提神的药酒。随后她低声要她的孩子：孩子抱来了：她把他放进父亲的怀里。“保护她，”她说，“并祝福你这垂死的——”

这句话没能说完，她跌回枕上：她的面容安详平静；父亲把婴儿贴在胸前时，她以坚定的目光望着他；一道突至的喜悦掠过她憔悴的眉眼：她举目望天——然后永远地阖上了它们。

## XXXIV Retribution / XXXIV 报应

===Original===

In the meantime, Montraville having received orders to return to New York, arrived, and having still some remains of compassionate tenderness for the woman whom he regarded as brought to shame by himself, he went out in search of Belcour, to inquire whether she was safe, and whether the child lived. He found him immersed in dissipation, and could gain no other intelligence than that Charlotte had left him, and that he knew not what was become of her.

“I can not believe it possible,” said Montraville, “that a mind once so pure as Charlotte Temple’s should so suddenly become the mansion of vice. Beware, Belcour,” continued he, “beware if you have dared to behave either unjustly or dishonorably to that poor girl, your life shall pay the forfeit:﻿—I will revenge her cause.”

He immediately went into the country, to the house where he had left Charlotte. It was desolate. After much inquiry he at length found the servant girl who had lived with her. From her he learnt the misery Charlotte had endured from the complicated evils of illness, poverty, and a broken heart, and that she had set out on foot for New York on a cold winter’s evening; but she could inform him no further.

Tortured almost to madness by this shocking account, he returned to the city, but before he reached it, the evening was drawing to a close. In entering the town, he was obliged to pass several little huts,19 the residences of poor women, who supported themselves by washing the clothes of the officers and soldiers. It was nearly dark; he heard from a neighboring steeple a solemn toll that seemed to say, some poor mortal was going to their last mansion: the sound struck on the heart of Montraville, and he involuntarily stopped, when from one of the houses he saw the appearance of a funeral. Almost unknowing what he did, he followed at a small distance; and as they let the coffin into the grave, he inquired of a soldier, who stood by, and had just brushed off a tear that did honor to his heart, who it was that was just buried. “An’ please your honor,” said the man, “ ’tis a poor girl that was brought from her friends by a cruel man, who left her when she was big with child, and married another.” Montraville stood motionless, and the man proceeded﻿—“I met her myself, not a fortnight since, one night, all wet and cold in the streets; she went to Madam Crayton’s, but she would not take her in and so the poor thing went raving mad.” Montraville could bear no more; he struck his hands against his forehead with violence, and exclaiming, “poor murdered Charlotte!” ran with precipitation towards the place where they were heaping the earth on her remains. “Hold﻿—hold! one moment,” said he, “close not the grave of the injured Charlotte Temple, till I have taken vengeance on her murderer.”

===Chinese===

与此同时，蒙特维尔奉调返回纽约，到了；他对那个他认为被自己带入耻辱的女子，心里还残存着几分怜惜的柔情，便出门去找贝尔库尔，打听她是否平安，孩子是否活着。他见贝尔库尔正沉溺在吃喝玩乐里，除了夏洛特已离开他、不知去向以外，什么消息也打听不到。

“我不能相信，”蒙特维尔说，“一颗曾经像夏洛特·坦普尔的心那样纯洁的心，会这样突然变成罪恶的宅邸。当心，贝尔库尔，”他接着说，“倘若你竟敢对那可怜的姑娘有半分不义或不光彩的行为，当心你的性命抵罪：——我要为她的冤屈复仇。”

他立刻下乡，去他安置夏洛特的那座屋子。那里一片荒凉。多方打听之后，他终于找到了从前伺候她的那个女仆。从她口中，他得知夏洛特在疾病、贫穷和心碎三重苦难交织下所受的苦，得知她在一个寒冷冬天的傍晚徒步去了纽约；可她也只能告诉他这些。

这骇人的消息把他折磨得几乎发狂，他赶回城里，还未到，暮色已经四合。进城时，他不得不经过几座小屋[19]——那是穷苦女人们的住处，靠替军官和士兵洗衣度日。天几乎黑了；他听见邻近教堂的钟楼传来庄严的钟声，像是在说，某个可怜的凡人正走向最后的居所：那声音叩在蒙特维尔的心上，他不由自主地停下脚步；这时他看见其中一座屋里出现了出殡的样子。他几乎不知道自己在做什么，远远地跟着；棺木落进墓穴时，他问一个站在旁边、刚刚拭去一滴足以给他的心增添光荣的眼泪的士兵：刚葬下的是谁。“禀告长官，”那人说，“是个可怜的姑娘，被一个狠心的男人从亲友身边带来的，她怀了身子他还丢下她，另娶了别人。”蒙特维尔呆立不动，那人接着说——“我自己就遇到过她，还不到两个星期前，一个夜里，她在街上，浑身湿透，冻得发抖；她到克雷顿夫人府上去，夫人不肯收留，那可怜的人就疯啦。”蒙特维尔再也受不住；他猛地以手击额，喊了一声“被害惨了的夏洛特！”便朝人们正在往她遗骸上堆土的地方狂奔而去。“住手——住手！等一等，”他说，“先别合上受屈的夏洛特·坦普尔的墓，等我为她的凶手报了仇。”

===Original===

“Rash young man,” said Mr. Temple, “who art thou that thus disturbest the last mournful rites of the dead, and rudely breakest in upon the grief of an afflicted father?”

“If thou art the father of Charlotte Temple,” said he, gazing at him with mingled horror and amazement﻿—“if thou art her father﻿—I am Montraville.”

Then, falling on his knees, he continued﻿—“Here is my bosom. I bare it to receive the stroke I merit. Strike﻿—strike now, and save me from the misery of reflection.”

“Alas!” said Mr. Temple, “if thou wert the seducer of my child, thy own reflections be thy punishment. I wrest not the power from the hand of Omnipotence. Look on that little heap of earth; there hast thou buried the only joy of a fond father. Look at it often; and may thy heart feel such true sorrow as shall merit the mercy of Heaven.” He turned from him, and Montraville, starting up from the ground where he had thrown himself, and at that instant remembering the perfidy of Belcour, flew like lightning to his lodgings. Belcour was intoxicated; Montraville impetuous; they fought, and the sword of the latter entered the heart of his adversary. He fell, and expired almost instantly. Montraville had received a slight wound: and, overcome with the agitation of his mind, and loss of blood, was carried in a state of insensibility to his distracted wife. A dangerous illness and obstinate delirium ensued, during which he raved incessantly for Charlotte: but a strong constitution and the tender assiduities of Julia, in time overcame the disorder. He recovered, but to the end of his life was subject to severe fits of melancholy, and while he remained at New York,20 frequently retired to the churchyard, where he would weep over the grave, and regret the untimely fate of the lovely Charlotte Temple.


===Chinese===

“鲁莽的年轻人，”坦普尔先生说，“你是谁，竟这样搅扰死者最后的哀仪，粗暴地闯进一个伤心父亲的悲哀？”

“你若是夏洛特·坦普尔的父亲，”他说，又惊又骇地凝视着他——“你若是她的父亲——我就是蒙特维尔。”

说罢，他跪倒在地，接着说——“这就是我的胸膛。我袒开它，来领我应得的一击。下手——现在就下手，把我从回忆的苦刑里解救出去吧。”

“唉！”坦普尔先生说，“你若真是我孩子的诱骗者，你自己的回忆便是你的刑罚。我不从全能者手中夺取那权柄。看那一小堆土吧；在那里，你埋葬了一个慈父惟一的欢乐。常常看看它；愿你的心感到真正的悲悔，好配得上上天的慈悲。”他转身而去；蒙特维尔从扑倒的地上跳起，那一瞬间想起贝尔库尔的背信，便闪电般飞回寓所。贝尔库尔醉着；蒙特维尔狂怒；两人交了手，后者的剑刺进了对手的心脏。他倒下，几乎当场断气。蒙特维尔受了一点轻伤：心神的激荡和失血使他失去了知觉，被人抬到他几近发狂的妻子跟前。随后是一场凶险的疾病和顽固的谵妄，病中他不停地喊着夏洛特：但强健的体魄加上朱莉娅的殷勤看护，终于战胜了病魔。他痊愈了，但直到生命的尽头，都时时被沉重的忧郁侵袭；他在纽约期间[20]，常常独自退到教堂墓地，在那座坟前哭泣，哀叹可爱的夏洛特·坦普尔的早夭。

## XXXV Conclusion / XXXV 结局

===Original===

Shortly after the interment of his daughter, Mr. Temple, with his dear little charge and her nurse, set forward for England. It would be impossible to do justice to the meeting scene between him, his Lucy, and her aged father. Every heart of sensibility can easily conceive their feelings. After the first tumult of grief was subsided, Mrs. Temple gave up the chief of her time to her grandchild, and as she grew up and improved, began to almost fancy she again possessed her Charlotte.

It was about ten years after these painful events, that Mr. and Mrs. Temple, having buried their father, were obliged to come to London on particular business,21 and brought the little Lucy with them. They had been walking one evening, when, on their return they found a poor wretch sitting on the steps of the door. She attempted to rise as they approached, but from extreme weakness was unable, and after several fruitless efforts fell back in a fit. Mr. Temple was not one of those men who stand to consider whether by assisting an object in distress they shall not inconvenience themselves, but, instigated by the impulse of a noble, feeling heart, immediately ordered her to be carried into the house and proper restoratives applied.

She soon recovered; and fixing her eyes on Mrs. Temple, cried﻿—“You know not, madam, what you do; you know not whom you are relieving, or you would curse me in the bitterness of your heart. Come not near me, madam, I shall contaminate you. I am the viper that stung your peace. I am the woman who turned the poor Charlotte out to perish in the street. Heaven have mercy! I see her now,” continued she, looking at Lucy; “such, such was the fair bud of innocence that my vile arts blasted ere it was half blown.”

It was in vain that Mr. and Mrs. Temple entreated her to be composed and to take some refreshment. She only drank half a glass of wine; and then told them that she had been separated from her husband seven years, the chief of which she had passed in riot, dissipation, and vice, till, overtaken by poverty and sickness, she had been reduced to part with every valuable, and thought only of ending her life in a prison; when a benevolent friend paid her debts and released her; but that, her illness increasing, she had no possible means of supporting herself, and her friends were weary of relieving her. “I have fasted,” said she, “two days, and last night laid my aching head on the cold pavement: indeed, it was but just that I should experience those miseries myself, which I had unfeelingly inflicted on others.”

Greatly as Mr. Temple had reason to detest Mrs. Crayton, he could not behold her in this distress without some emotions of pity. He gave her shelter that night beneath his hospitable roof, and the next day got her admission into an hospital: where, having lingered a few weeks, she died, a striking example that vice, however prosperous in the beginning, in the end leads only to misery and shame.


===Chinese===

安葬了女儿不久，坦普尔先生便带着他亲爱的小宝贝和她的奶妈启程回英格兰。他与他的露西和她年迈的父亲重逢的那一幕，想写得公平允当是不可能的。每一颗敏感的心都不难想见他们的感受。第一阵悲恸的风暴平息之后，坦普尔夫人把大半光阴都给了她的外孙女；孩子渐渐长大、日见长进，她几乎开始觉得，她又重新拥有了自己的夏洛特。

这些伤心事之后约十年，坦普尔先生和夫人——他们已安葬了父亲——因一件特别的公务不得不同赴伦敦[21]，也把小露西带在身边。一天傍晚他们散步归来，发现门阶上坐着一个可怜的人。见他们走近，她想站起来，却因极度虚弱站不起，挣扎了几次，徒劳无功，向后一晕倒下。坦普尔先生不是那种先掂量救人会不会给自己添麻烦的人；他受一颗高贵善感之心的驱使，立刻吩咐把她抬进屋去，用上适当的醒药剂。

她很快醒了过来；眼睛盯住坦普尔夫人，喊道——“您不知道，夫人，您做的是什么事；您不知道您救济的是什么人，否则您会从心底里咒骂我。别靠近我，夫人，我会玷污您。我是那条咬碎您安宁的毒蛇。我就是把可怜的夏洛特撵出去死在街上的那个女人。上天垂怜！我如今看见她了，”她望着露西，接着说；“那朵纯洁的蓓蕾正是这样，还没半开，就叫我卑污的手段摧残了。”

坦普尔先生和夫人劝她镇静、用些饮食，都是徒然。她只喝了半杯酒；随后告诉他们，她同丈夫分离已经七年，其中大部分光阴都耗在放纵、挥霍和罪恶里，直到贫穷和疾病追上她，逼得她变卖了每一件值钱的东西，只想着在监狱里了却残生；这时一位仁厚的朋友替她还了债，放了她出来；可是病情加重，她再无生计可谋，朋友们也接济得厌了。“我已经，”她说，“两天没吃东西了，昨夜把我疼痛的头枕在冰冷的路面上：真的，我自己曾无情地加于别人的苦难，如今该我自己尝一尝，这再公道不过。”

尽管坦普尔先生有十足的理由憎恶克雷顿夫人，眼见她落到这般田地，也不能不生出几分怜悯。那一夜他在好客的屋顶下收留了她，第二天设法把她送进了一家医院：她在那里捱了几个星期，死去了——一个触目的例证：罪恶纵然开局得意，到头来只会通向苦难与羞辱。

There is something fine in a romance which makes of the man who thus brought together Henry Temple and Lucy Eldridge, the benefactor, a quarter of a century afterward, of the daughter of their unfortunate child, Charlotte. The word “old” in this paragraph does not appear in late editions. It is now restored to its place from the original American text. When Mrs. Rowson was writing her story, the living Earl of Derby had held the title about thirteen years, and was then thirty-seven years old. The “old Earl” was his grandfather, Edward Stanley, who had held the title forty-two years, and died in 1776, at the age of eighty-seven. One of the “old Earl’s” daughters, named Charlotte, was the wife of General Burgoyne, of the Revolution. The Charlotte Stanley who is believed to have been buried in Trinity churchyard appears, therefore, to have borne the name of her father’s sister. See Burke’s “Peerage.” ↩︎

The identity of the house which Charlotte was now leaving with a house shown on the Ratzen map has already been referred to in the Introduction. It is interesting to note further than in Watson’s “Annals,” published in 1846, its location is given as what was then No. 24 Bowery, the edifice being described as “a low wooden house.” Watson gives Dr. John W. Francis, the author of “Old New York,” as his authority for the statement that Charlotte lived in this house. Dr. Francis, at the time when Watson wrote, was 57 years old, and had spent his life in New York, where he was born in 1789.

The Bowery at that point is now accessible from the west, not only by Pell Street, but by another street, called Doyers, which turns northerly and soon enters Pell, thus making a small triangular block bounded by Doyers, Pell, and the Bowery. Within this enclosure originally stood the two houses shown on the Ratzen map, Charlotte’s house being subsequently removed to the northwest corner of Pell and the Bowery, where, as already stated, it was known as “The Old Tree House.” ↩︎

夏洛特此刻离开的这座屋子，同拉岑地图上标示的那座房屋的同一性，已在前言里说过了。还有一点有趣：据华生 1846 年出版的《编年史》，其位置在当时纽约的 No. 24 包厘街（Bowery），那房子被描述为“一座低矮的木屋”。华生引《老纽约》的作者约翰·W·弗朗西斯医生（Dr. John W. Francis）的话为据，说夏洛特就住在这座屋里。华生执笔时，弗朗西斯医生 57 岁，一生都在纽约度过，他 1789 年生于该城。

包厘街的这一段，如今从西侧进去，不只可经佩尔街，还可经另一条叫多耶斯的街，它折向北，很快并入佩尔街，于是围出一个小三角街区，以多耶斯、佩尔和包厘三条街为界。这片围地里原先立着拉岑地图上的那两座房子，夏洛特住的那座后来移到佩尔街与包厘街的西北角，如前所述，它以“老树屋”之名为人所知。↩︎

===Original===

In attempts heretofore made to establish the identity of this house, two famous Colonial homes have been brought into the discussion﻿—the Franklin and the Walton. The former was perhaps first suggested in consequence of its name, but, as already pointed out, the Julia Franklin episode in “Charlotte Temple” never occurred in real life.

The Franklin house stood at the northwest corner of Franklin Square and Cherry Street, the site being now overshadowed by one of the arches of the approach to the Brooklyn Bridge. It was built in 1770, and few, if any, private houses in America at that time, were more imposing. During Washington’s residence in New York as President, beginning in 1789, it was his first home.

The Walton house, of which the Franklin house was a rival, stood a little further south on Pearl Street, near Franklin Square, and had been built twenty years earlier, when no home in America was quite its equal in architectural splendor or in furnishings. Its owner, William Walton, was a commercial magnate who, in the late Colonial times, entertained with such exceptional munificence, that his expenditures were cited in Parliament as evidence of the ability of people in the Colonies to bear the burden of the Stamp Tax. ↩︎

- 
					

===Chinese===

在历来考证这座房屋身份的尝试中，有两座著名的殖民地宅邸被引入讨论——富兰克林宅和沃尔顿宅。前者之所以被首先提出，或许是因为名字，但如前所指出的，《夏洛特·坦普尔》中朱莉娅·富兰克林一段从未在现实中发生过。

富兰克林宅位于富兰克林广场与樱桃街的西北角，如今那里被布鲁克林大桥引桥的一孔拱影所笼罩。它建于 1770 年，当时美国的私宅，少有比它更气派的。华盛顿 1789 年开始以总统身份驻节纽约期间，它便是他的第一处居所。

与富兰克林宅竞胜的沃尔顿宅，坐落在更南面的珍珠街上，近富兰克林广场，早建了二十年，当年在美国，论建筑的华丽与陈设的精美，没有一处宅邸能与它匹敌。它的主人威廉·沃尔顿（William Walton）是位商业巨子，殖民地晚期，他款待宾客的豪奢不同寻常，以致他的开销被拿到议会里引证，用来说明殖民地人有力承担印花税的重负。↩︎

===Original===

Mrs. Rowson, in “Lucy Temple,” says Colonel Franklin (that is, Montraville) “returned to his own country, which he had left nine years before a captain of artillery, with little besides his pay, an honorable descent, and fair character, to receive the thanks of royalty for his intrepidity [an honor which, as a matter of fact, Colonel Montrésor is known to have received], and to dash into the world of splendor and gaiety. Promoted to the rank of colonel of artillery, and having had the office of Chief Engineer during his service abroad [the exact office, be it remembered, which Colonel Montrésor held in America], he stood in an elevated rank and associated with the first personages in the kingdom.” After Colonel Franklin’s early death, his widow, discontented in England, “embarked for New York with the whole of her family,” and later “purchased a beautiful seat on the banks of the Delaware,” where she continued to live “in the enjoyment of all the happiness which was to be derived from the society of her family and the delightful serenity of nature.” ↩︎

- 
					

In “Lucy Temple” the death of Colonel Blakeney is said to have occurred when Lucy was ten years old. By the “particular business” above referred to, Mrs. Rowson may have had in mind the settlement of his will under which Lucy came into possession of £20,000.

Lieutenant Colonel Grice Blakeney, of the British army, who died about 1785, as already stated in the Introduction, has been identified as the original of the Blakeney of “Charlotte Temple” and “Lucy Temple.” He belonged to an ancient English family long settled in Norfolk, where they possessed a considerable landed estate, but in the reign of Queen Elizabeth removed to Ireland. In Galway they still have their seat, which is called Castle Blakeney. Colonel Blakeney’s direct connection with the family in Ireland is indicated in Burke’s “Landed Gentry.” He is described there as an army officer “who died unmarried.” ↩︎


===Chinese===

罗森夫人在《露西·坦普尔》中说，富兰克林上校（即蒙特维尔）“回到了自己的国家；九年前他离开时还是一名炮兵上尉，除薪俸外别无长物，只有体面的家世和清白的名声；此番回来，接受王室对他骁勇的嘉奖（事实上，据知蒙特雷索上校曾获此项荣誉），一头扎进浮华欢场的上流世界。他已晋升炮兵上校，并且在海外服役期间担任过总工程师之职（请记住，这正是蒙特雷索上校在美洲担任的职务），地位显赫，与王国的一流人物过从。”富兰克林上校早逝之后，他的遗孀在英格兰心怀不满，“携全家登船去了纽约”，后来“在特拉华河畔买了一处美丽的宅邸”，在那里继续生活，“享受家人团聚之乐和大自然的恬静所给予的一切幸福”。↩︎

- 

在《露西·坦普尔》中，布莱克尼上校之死说是发生在露西十岁的时候。上文所说的“特别的公务”，罗森夫人心中所想的，或许是他遗嘱的清结——露西由此继承了 20,000 镑财产。

英军中那位约死于 1785 年的格里斯·布莱克尼中校（Lieutenant Colonel Grice Blakeney），如前言所述，已被认定为《夏洛特·坦普尔》和《露西·坦普尔》中布莱克尼的原型。他属于一个久居诺福克的古老英格兰家族，在那里拥有相当的地产，但在伊丽莎白女王治下迁往爱尔兰。在戈尔韦，他们至今仍有宅邸，名为布莱克尼城堡。布莱克尼上校与爱尔兰这一家族的直系关联，见于伯克的《地主绅士谱》。他在那里被描述为一名“死后未婚”的军官。↩︎

## The Author’s Preface / 作者序

===Original===

For the perusal of the young and thoughtless of the fair sex, this Tale of Truth is designed; and I could wish my fair readers to consider it as not merely the effusion of Fancy, but as a reality. The circumstances on which I have founded this novel were related to me some little time since by an old lady who had personally known Charlotte, though she concealed the real names of the characters, and likewise the place where the unfortunate scenes were acted: yet, as it was impossible to offer a relation to the public in such an imperfect state, I have thrown over the whole a slight veil of fiction, and substituted names and places according to my own fancy. The principal characters in this little tale are now consigned to the silent tomb: it can therefore hurt the feelings of no one, and may, I flatter myself, be of service to some who are so unfortunate as to have neither friends to advise or understanding to direct them through the various and unexpected evils that attend a young and unprotected woman in her first entrance into life.

While the tear of compassion still trembled in my eye for the fate of the unhappy Charlotte, I may have children of my own, said I, to whom this recital may be of use, and if to your own children, said Benevolence, why not to the many daughters of Misfortune who, deprived of natural friends or spoilt by a mistaken education, are thrown on an unfeeling world without the least power to defend themselves from the snares, not only of the other sex, but from the more dangerous arts of the profligate of their own?

Sensible as I am that a novel writer, at a time when such a variety of works are ushered into the world under that name, stands but a poor chance for fame in the annals of literature, but conscious that I wrote with a mind anxious for the happiness of that sex whose morals and conduct have so powerful an influence on mankind in general; and convinced that I have not wrote a line that conveys a wrong idea to the head, or a corrupt wish to the heart, I shall rest satisfied in the purity of my own intentions, and if I merit not applause, I feel that I dread not censure.

If the following tale should save one hapless fair one from the errors which ruined poor Charlotte, or rescue from impending misery the heart of one anxious parent, I shall feel a much higher gratification in reflecting on this trifling performance than could possibly result from the applause which might attend the most elegant, finished piece of literature whose tendency might deprave the heart or mislead the understanding.


===Chinese===

这篇真实的故事，是为女性中年少而少思虑的人写的；我愿我可爱的读者们不把它仅仅看作想象的产物，而看作一件真事。我这部小说所本的情节，是不久前一位老夫人向我讲述的，她亲识夏洛特其人，只是隐去了人物的真实姓名，也隐去了那不幸的各幕上演的地点：然而，以那样不完备的面貌把一段叙述公之于世是不可能的，我便给全篇罩上一层薄薄的小说之纱，并按自己的意思替换了姓名和地点。这个小故事里的主要人物如今都已归于寂静的坟墓：它因此不可能伤任何人的感情，而且，恕我自诩，或许还能对一些人有好处——她们不幸既没有朋友指教，也没有见识引导，去应付一个年轻而无保护的女子初入人生时随之而来的种种不虞之祸。

我眼中还悬着为不幸的夏洛特的命运而颤动的同情之泪，我对自己说，我自己或许也会有儿女，这段叙述于他们或有用处；“若于你自己的儿女有用，”“仁善”说，“为什么不可以用于许许多多‘不幸’的女儿们呢？——她们或被夺去了天生的亲友，或被错误的教育惯坏，被抛到一个冷酷的世上，毫无自卫之力，既要防异性的圈套，更要防她们同性中放荡之徒那更危险的手段。”

我深知，在这样一个以小说之名问世的著作纷然杂陈的时代，一个写小说的人在文学编年史里博得名声的机会微乎其微；但我也自知，我写作时一心挂念的是那个其道德与品行对全人类影响至巨的性别的幸福；并且我确信，我没有写过一行字，会把错误的观念传给头脑，或把腐化的愿望传给心灵；那么，我便满足于自己意图的纯洁；倘若我不配掌声，我自觉也不惧非难。

倘若下面这个故事能使一个不幸的姑娘免于葬送可怜的夏洛特的那些过错，或把一位忧心父母的心从临头的苦难中解救出来，那么回望这篇微末之作时我所感到的快慰，将远非伴随最典雅完美的文学作品而来的掌声所能比拟——倘若那作品的倾向会败坏人心、误导理解的话。
